</ol>


<h4>How To Not Tell a Fable</h4>


<p>The classifiers are hella annoying sometimes, but Anthropic says they work.</p>
<blockquote><p>In all cases, Claude Haiku, Sonnet, and Opus models were used; no malicious activity was found on Claude Fable or Mythos (which has a series of safeguards in place that greatly reduce its ability to perform harmful cyber tasks).</p></blockquote>
<p>The one exception was an attempted distillation attack on Fable by Zhipu, but there were enhanced safeguards in place and Zhipu switched to going after Opus.</p>


<h4>Bad Dudes Tend To Be Relatively Unsophisticated</h4>


<p>If Bad Dudes were more often sophisticated and Good At Job, the world would look very different.</p>
<p>Luckily, Bad Dudes are usually unsophisticated and Bad At Job.</p>
<p>(Everyone else is mostly similar, but less so and less reliably.)</p>
<p>AI, for both better and worse, turns Bad At Job into Good At Job, making it less relevant that the humans are Bad At Job via doing the things. Here are Anthropic’s big themes:</p>
<ol>
<li>Sophisticated attacks no longer require sophisticated attackers.</li>
<li>AI’s role in cyber operations has become increasingly autonomous.</li>
<li>AI supply chain as target, loot, and attack compute.
<ol>
<li>As in, attackers go after sources of compute, and use that to keep going.</li>
<li>To use Claude as Bad Dude, you need the value of the loot and compute, and you also need the cover of newly compromised accounts, to stay ahead of Anthropic cracking down.</li>
<li>Basically everyone in the report is stealing their access, at minimum via getting around regional restrictions and buying endless subsidized subscriptions, on top of then doing Bad Dude things with the compute.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>AI tradecraft is proliferating.
<ol>
<li>Like everything else, diffusion takes time.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>
<p>You need fewer less sophisticated humans, who are less Good at Job, to Do Thing.</p>
<p>For most things, that’s great. This report is about the exceptions.</p>
<p>You especially need less in order to adapt when someone defends against you, and to develop new methods.</p>


<h4>Particular Bad Dudes</h4>


<p>GTG-20006 is a Russian espionage operation linked to Midnight Blizzard. They had a standard set of cyber attack tools. They used AI to monitor how well their tools evaded detection, and iterated until security defenses did not detect their malware, then launched AI-automated attacks, including AI phishing operations from AI-registered domains.</p>
<p>Targets varied, including Ukrainian government, military and diplomatic staff, various government agencies and defense-industrial companies linked to Ukraine, and Ukraine’s drone supply chain. They took over WhatsApp accounts via headless browsers and targeted surveillance cameras. All of it was automated.</p>
<p>GTG-50014 were smash-and-grab opportunists associated with the ShinyHunters collective. They are doing the standard opportunistic things, but AI let them scale and cast a wide net looking for vulnerable systems, targeting bulk data theft and potential extortion. The whole thing is basically ‘vibe hacking,’ with ‘living off the land,’ having the AI look around and use whatever it finds, seeking any vulnerability at all, rather than having a plan.</p>
<p>GTG-10007 was a Chinese-speaking espionage operation, likely in Changsha in China’s Hunan province. Claude was used to automate workflows and form agent swarms, the same as any other coding task, except here it was vulnerability research, testing and exploit design. Roughly fifty organizations were targeted, and an education-technology company was compromised.</p>
<p>GTG-50020 is a Russian-speaking, financially-motivated actor, historically targeting hotel booking and financial technology platforms, that pivoted to attack the AI industry and steal API keys via prompt injecting sandboxes. They attacked 30 companies, but they never achieved their objective, which was access to a pre-release Claude model.</p>
<p>GTG-50029 was a French-speaking hacktivist who targeted European political and affiliated entities, an example of uplift for unsophisticated threat actors, who then managed to compromise 14 of 42 WordPress sites including via an undocumented race condition and steal users’ political opinions, a mass attack on privacy.</p>


<h4>Influence Operations</h4>


<p>Influence operations are getting larger and more sophisticated.</p>
<blockquote><p>We’ve seen groups of actors use Claude to build networks of fake social media profiles and entire news sites, leveraging these platforms to publish deceptive content, while completely concealing the entities behind these operations.</p></blockquote>
<p>It is remarkable how well social media has held up so far in the age of AI. Threat actors spin up hundreds of accounts on a regular basis, all providing social proof for each other, and we complain but the defenders are mostly winning.</p>
<blockquote><p>This report details nine of those cases. They originated in Russia, Iran, Turkey, and<br />
across the Gulf, South Asia, Africa and Europe, and targeted audiences on six<br />
continents.</p></blockquote>
<p>Building such campaigns builds a signature that Anthropic often detects. When Anthropic finds an operation in the planning stage, or discovers one afterwards, they ban the accounts and use this to strengthen their detection mechanisms. But of course such actors can always get new accounts and try again.</p>
<p>Trends listed:</p>
<ol>
<li>Influence sold as a service.</li>
<li>AI as a newsdesk.</li>
<li>AI helped to build the apparatus as well as the content.</li>
<li>Complex tool use.</li>
<li>Laundering of attribution, sourcing and certainty.</li>
<li>Increased operational security.</li>
<li>Fake personas (and impersonation of real personas).</li>
<li>Targeting people and accountability mechanisms.</li>
<li>Influence operations often fail to reach a genuine audience.</li>
</ol>
<p>The final takeaway is what I see from the outside. These campaigns are often remarkably ineffective, individually and in general, and much more boogeymen so far than actually influential. Hopefully that will last.</p>
<p>These operations mostly (but not entirely) target third world areas, where there is less competition, that is less sophisticated, in the media and influence ecosystems.</p>
<p>They list nine:</p>
<ol>
<li>GTG-04001: Russian manipulation in the Central African Republic, via a heavily biased AI-generated news pipeline.</li>
<li>GTG-54002: Commercial ‘influence-as-a-service’ spanning six continents, traced to France. They used 70 fabricated news sites and 250 inauthentic Twitter accounts. Used Claude to write and rewrite news articles and tailor them.</li>
<li>GTG-84005: An election-manipulation platform targeting Malaysia. About 1,000 fake Twitter accounts, a fake news outlet and a series of fabricated dossiers, as paid political influence-as-service.</li>
<li>GTG-24015: Russian state-media editorial pipelines for editorial and news production for audiences in various countries. They targeted the elections in Moldova.</li>
<li>GTG-34001: Iranian state-aligned ICCO, Islamic Propaganda Office and Bina Observatory. They were laying the foundation for an explanatory jihad.</li>
<li>GTG-54006: Automated pro-Awami League self-described fake-news operation targeting rural Bangladesh.</li>
<li>GTG-84006: MEK/NCRI-aligned influence operation using AI to impersonate an activist and recruit inside Iran. They scraped posts on Telegram to try and assemble a profile of the particular activist.</li>
<li>GTG-54004: A domestic inauthentic behavior campaign in Kenya, basically astroturfing public sentiment.</li>
<li>GTG-84002: A UAE-directed influence operation targeting the Muslim Brotherhood, Sudan conflict and UN accountability mechanisms. This was supposed to be ‘a coordinated transatlantic and regional operation to dismantle the Muslim Brotherhood globally.’</li>
</ol>
<p>Overall, I was not impressed. It does not seem like such folks are getting much done, at least not via Claude. There’s a thin line between a lot of this and Ordinary Politics. If this is as bad as it gets then this is very good news.</p>


<h4>Surveillance Operations</h4>


<blockquote><p>These cases include threat actors from China, Iran, and West Africa, as well as the commercial “surveillance-for-hire” market, and range from operations carried out by a single individual to entire teams.</p></blockquote>
<p>And they did it in violation of the Terms of Service. How dare they.</p>
<p>In many cases this was ‘analyze a ton of social media posts,’ including by the state to target dissidents and dissident groups, or groups likely to cause trouble. Most of the groups were Chinese or Iranian. In one case the Iranians targeted Jews. Sometimes malware was involved.</p>
<p>Are Iran and China the only places trying to do this level of AI surveillance? Or are they the only ones crazy enough to use Claude?</p>
<p>Anthropic highlights GTG-50027, a single independent consultant in Bamako, who used Claude as part of Mali’s state intelligence service (ANSE) to target roughly 25 million SIM cards. This one was not really disrupted. Further work was prevented, but the system had already been deployed and it remains deployed.</p>
<p>This is fundamentally not a solvable problem. Anthropic and other AI companies are in the business of selling intelligence, and there are also open models providing intelligence. There is no way to fully stop all forms of mass surveillance, domestically or otherwise, given the alternative options. Claude does not provide that big an advantage here over open models. If some people are out there to analyze public information, you can slow them down but they are going to succeed.</p>
<p>Another operation stood out: GTG-30005, due to its target:</p>
<blockquote><p>In another investigation, we identified and disrupted an Iran-nexus threat actor that used Claude to collect and analyze publicly accessible data to develop targeting<br />
recommendations against US naval forces in the region.</p></blockquote>
<p>That goes hand in hand with the next section: Use of Claude in conventional weapons.</p>


<h4>Conventional Weapons</h4>


<p>This refers to software for such weapons, as well as targeting and control systems. There are six cases here: three in China, two in Russia and one in Yemen.</p>
<p>First there are the weapon systems development cases:</p>
<ol>
<li>GTG-87001: A Yemen-based guided weapons engineering cell using Claude to develop guidance software. Claude as software engineer, except for weapons.</li>
<li>GTG-17001: A China-based operation drafting a fire control specification and acquisition documents for undersea warfare, within their defense contractor ecosystem. They pretended to be American to get Claude to write a proposal.</li>
<li>GTG-27005: A Russia-based likely freelance operation to engineer an autonomous military first-person-view kamikaze drone swarm. Again, the wrong software.</li>
<li>GTG-17002: A China-based operation to build targeting software for electronic warfare and air defense suppression.</li>
</ol>
<p>It is a thin line between ordinary software development, and software that assists with conventional weapons. It is no surprise developers would attempt to use Claude. Indeed, presumably lots of Western weapons developers also use Claude.</p>
<ol>
<li>GTG-27006: Russia-based operation to procure mixed military and civilian goods. Claude was used to buy things. Eventually it added up to obvious military use.</li>
<li>GTG-17003: China-based operation to collect open-source intelligence on directed-energy weapons and their supply chain.</li>
</ol>
<p>Again, this happens to be a particular use we do not like, which is Not So Different.</p>


<h4>Biological Misuse</h4>


<p>How much is being attempted in practice? They have five cases.</p>
<p>Regional use controls were evaded, but the five case studies here look like scientists. They are doing dual use things, where it is not clear that harm was intended.</p>
<ol>
<li>A grant application for gain-of-function research.</li>
<li>A research program engineering highly pathogenic mammal-adapted avian influenza.</li>
<li>Orthopoxvirus research, including help with related logistics.</li>
<li>Two cases of dual-use non-transmissible novel venoms and toxins.</li>
</ol>
<p>The first two cases seem like clear cases of Do Not Want, even if the people involved thought they were helping. The other three are less clear. None of the cases were the kind of dangerous use of AI we worry about, where AI provides major uplift. AI was being used because AI is highly useful at ordinary tasks.</p>
<p>The New York Times report on this, with the headline ‘<a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/10/us/politics/anthropic-ai-biological-weapons.html?smid=nytcore-ios-share&amp;referringSource=articleShare">Anthropic Says It Blocked Possible Efforts To Build Biological Weapons</a>,’ seems highly overstated here.</p>
<p>I call this a huge success story, assuming Anthropic isn’t hiding worse things. These aren’t even fully Bad Dudes, only misguided ones.</p>


<h4>Scams and Fraud</h4>


<p>Ah, good to get back to Ordinary Decent Crime. Except, you know, at scale.</p>
<p>They only offer one example, but it’s a fun one.</p>
<p>GTG-15001: Fake dating app network. Love it. Now we’re talking. To AIs. A full network of 20 fake dating apps, with more than 4,700 distinct AI personas that talked to over 25,000 unique individuals over two weeks in April, with swipe feeds that were 25% real people and 75% AIs. For some of the 25%, workers were hired to choose among three candidate AI replies, thus proving good game design, and paid per message. Claude ran the AI personas, because nothing but the best will do. I might be mangling the details a bit.</p>
<p>Messaging is metered, which is how they make money. That was a hint.</p>
<p>I mean, yeah, yeah, scam and fraud, poor fake users, very sad.</p>
<p>But this is clearly the best case study.</p>
<p>The weird part is this is the best case study, and here the only one. Where are all the others? Again this is a huge success story.</p>


<h4>Illicit Fraudulent Distillation</h4>


<p>This is the controversial one.</p>
<blockquote><p>We define illicit distillation as an industrial-scale, covert campaign to extract a model’s capabilities and replicate them in another model without authorization. Illicit distillation is typically enabled by fraud: sophisticated networks of fake accounts created with stolen credit cards, login credentials, and API keys.</p>
<p>Other frontier labs have faced distillation attacks. OpenAI has called attention to this activity since early 2025.</p></blockquote>
<p>If you want to argue that distillation using your own queries and the model outputs, or data otherwise acquired above board, is fine, I respectfully disagree with you for practical reasons and for the same reasons I support copyright and patent protections, but I understand why you would think that.</p>
<p>If you think that it is okay to then use a ‘Chain of Thought extractor’ as an exploit, as part of that effort, I disagree with you a lot more, and I think you’re clearly in the wrong, but I understand why some people think intellectual property is not real and you should be able to just steal it and are willing to cheer for Chinese companies to steal American IP.</p>
<p>If you think it is okay to do this by secretly rerouting sensitive customer queries to Claude, or using accounts created with stolen credit cards, login credentials and API keys to harvest data?</p>
<p>Then I’m sorry, you’re just flat out wrong, that is obviously not okay.</p>
<p>I mention those particular things because that is what is going on.</p>
<blockquote><p>Over the last several months, unauthorized labs have developed increasingly<br />
sophisticated methods to circumvent our defenses and harvest the capabilities of US frontier models. These labs generally access Anthropic’s models by routing requests through proxy services, also known as “transfer stations.”</p>
<p>To circumvent our geographic restrictions and related controls, these proxy services create thousands of new accounts using false identities, fake or stolen credit cards, and stolen API keys.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>Unauthorized labs also obtain transcripts of user exchanges with US frontier models by purchasing them from third-party resellers. These resellers include the operators of proxy services, which often save exchanges between users and US models without the knowledge or consent of those users.</p></blockquote>
<p>Meanwhile, there is an ongoing war where Anthropic tries to block various prompts that attempt to extract the Chain of Thought, and the Chinese and other attackers try to develop new extraction methods.</p>
<p>Also, it gets worse:</p>
<blockquote><p>These findings raise concerns about the misuse of user data by PRC AI<br />
labs. DeepSeek, Xiaomi, and Moonshot fed conversations between their own models and users into Claude.</p>
<p>These labs then used Claude’s responses as training data with which to distill Claude’s capabilities. Some of these exchanges included sensitive information, including from individual users, major multinational companies, and state-affiliated actors. Many of these exchanges were relayed from users of third-party model routing services commonly used by users in the United States and Europe. Those sessions contained names, email addresses, company data, and other sensitive data of hundreds of end users in at least a dozen languages. These practices are likely inconsistent with privacy laws and the labs’ own terms of service.</p></blockquote>
<p>The report includes the example of asking Claude to analyze CCTV surveillance footage from hundreds of Chinese cameras, and exposing live Russian government credentials. It can get ugly out there.</p>
<p>Your response can be ‘well Anthropic is flat out lying about this’ but short of that there is no way to excuse the behaviors in question.</p>
<p>All of this looks like it is in direct violation of numerous laws, including Chinese laws.</p>
<p>In many of these cases, if nothing else, Article 39 of PIPL was definitely broken by DeepSeek, Moonshot and Xiaomi, and likely Criminal Law Art. 235a as well:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://personalinformationprotectionlaw.com/PIPL/article-39/">Accessible Law</a> (PIPL): Where a personal information processor provides personal information of an individual to a party outside the territory of the People’s Republic of China, it shall inform the individual of such matters as the name of the overseas recipient, contact information, purpose, and method of processing, type of personal information and the way and procedure for the individual to exercise the rights prescribed herein against the overseas recipient, and shall obtain the individual’s separate consent.</p></blockquote>
<p>That’s in addition to everyone breaking the Anti-Unfair Competition Law, of using data lawfully held by another business operator through “fraud, coercion, or circumventing or damaging technical or management measures” where this disrupts market competition order. Seems to apply here.</p>
<p>Oh, and all of this involved Ordinary Decent Fraud, as in Criminal Law Art. 196 and Art. 177a.</p>
<p>And then there’s Interim Measures for the Management of Generative AI Services, Article 7.</p>
<p>There are six particular cases.</p>
<ol>
<li>Alibaba (Qwen) used a massive network of fake accounts.</li>
<li>Moonshot (Kimi) secretly sent user exchanges to Claude, via a massive network of fake accounts.</li>
<li>DeepSeek also secretly sent user exchanges to Claude.</li>
<li>Zhipu (GLM) did the extraction thing, and the fake account thing, and also recently went after the cyber capabilities of leading American models ahead of the release of GLM-5.3.</li>
<li>Xiaomi did the distillation thing, using saved user requests.</li>
<li>SenseTime, MiniMax and others form a third-party reseller ecosystem.</li>
</ol>
<p>As in, all the cool kids are doing it. That’s how they are the Chinese cool kids.</p>
<p>How do the Chinese have the best open models?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/qSjH9T83xCfWQkmk2/mujbpe6zlankbuga4oh4" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>GTG-16005: CoT distillation and AI R&amp;D campaign by Alibaba (Qwen / Tongyi Lab).</p>
<blockquote><p>Alibaba’s CoT distillation pipeline injected a fixed prompt into each request that forced Claude to write out its reasoning traces inside inline text tags before providing its final answer. Those CoT transcripts were then saved and converted into data that could be used for supervised fine-tuning (SFT). These SFT transcripts were used to help train Alibaba’s Qwen models, and were used to distill Claude’s capabilities into Qwen 3.5, 3.6, and 3.7.</p>
<p>Alibaba’s illicit distillation campaign peaked at nearly 3 million exchanges per day<br />
launched from more than 3,500 fraudulent accounts.</p>
<p>… Scale of distillation attacks attributable to Alibaba between May and July 2026: over 151 million exchanges observed.</p></blockquote>
<p>GTG-16002: Moonshot serves Claude instead of Kimi and collects exchanges for model training.</p>
<blockquote><p>We discovered that Moonshot AI, the company that produces the Kimi family of<br />
models, silently forwarded customer requests to Claude, instead of processing them<br />
using Kimi. Moonshot then displayed Claude’s responses to users. These users thought they were using a Kimi model, but received responses from Claude instead.</p>
<p>In one instance, over a ten-day period, Moonshot relayed almost 300,000 customer<br />
requests to Anthropic, the vast majority of which were routed to Opus. Moonshot used a proxy service network of 5,380 fraudulent accounts, most of which appeared to be located in Singapore and Japan.</p>
<p>In addition to serving Claude’s responses to their customers, Moonshot also captured and saved at least a portion of these exchanges.</p>
<p>… When responding, Claude returns a reference to its raw thinking as a<br />
“thinking signature” instead of the raw thinking to mitigate the risk of unauthorized distillation.</p>
<p>… Scale of distillation attacks attributable to Moonshot between May and July 2026: over 23 million exchanges observed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lymanstoneky/status/2098115643505811693">Lyman Stone 石來民</a>: Okay so over the course of reading this I shifted from “wow Kimi is awful” to “WHY DID ANTHROPIC SPILL THE BEANS ON THIS INCREDIBLE INTELLIGENCE HACK”</p></blockquote>
<p>No, Lyman. I get why you would say that, but we do not steal user data, even if the user was attempting to use Kimi and kind of deserves it.</p>
<p>GTG-16001: DeepSeek serves Claude instead of its own models and collects exchanges for model training.</p>
<blockquote><p>Our investigation revealed that DeepSeek also deployed tactics similar to Moonshot’s. DeepSeek built a CoT extraction pipeline, relying on the same cross-session replay attack described above. DeepSeek also silently relayed exchanges to Claude without informing DeepSeek customers. Like GTG-16002, their customers were likely not made aware that their requests were being funneled to Claude.</p>
<p>… Scale of distillation attacks attributable to DeepSeek over 14 days in July 2026: over 12.1 million exchanges observed.</p></blockquote>
<p>GTG-16006: Zhipu distillation, AI R&amp;D and targeting cyber capabilities.</p>
<blockquote><p>Zhipu, branded outside China as Z.ai, ran a chain-of-thought extraction pipeline against Claude, replaying captured Claude reasoning traces back through Claude to clean them for training its GLM models. Over just ten days, Zhipu launched a CoT extraction pipeline against Claude Opus 4.8 by rotating through 273 fraudulent accounts to evade our model restrictions.</p>
<p>… Zhipu also used Claude to improve its own post-training pipelines, using Claude to judge model outputs and clean and normalize reasoning transcripts harvested for<br />
distillation.</p>
<p>… More recently, ahead of the release of its GLM 5.3 model, we identified a campaign to target the cyber capabilities of leading US frontier models. Zhipu researchers used public vulnerability datasets to develop various capture-the-flag challenges.</p>
<p>Zhipu initially attempted to target the cyber capabilities of Anthropic’s Fable model. Fable—Anthropic’s top generally accessible model—has strengthened cyber<br />
safeguards, making it more difficult for would-be distillers to target Fable’s cyber<br />
capabilities. Zhipu eventually gave up trying to target Fable after Anthropic’s cyber<br />
safeguards degraded Zhipu’s attacks.</p>
<p>… Scale of distillation attacks attributable to Zhipu over 17 days in June and July 2026: over 3.4 million exchanges observed.</p></blockquote>
<p>That’s right. They tried to forcibly distill Fable’s cyber capabilities in order to put them into an open model. Luckily, this did not work and they had to pivot to Opus 4.6 where the safeguards were weaker.</p>
<p>GTG-16008: Distillation campaign by Xiaomi</p>
<p>This one was only ~400k messages, using saved user messages to seed the exchanges.</p>
<blockquote><p>Our investigation suggests that Xiaomi may have launched its MiMo-V2-Pro model<br />
with a free trial period—which was then extended—with the intent to use the surge in international developer use of the model to distill Claude capabilities. The bulk of the distillation attacks on Claude began just as the trial period was ending.</p></blockquote>
<p>Distilling Claude is the business model.</p>


<h4>What You Gonna Do About It, Punk?</h4>


<p>The arms race continues. If you’re wondering why you can’t see the CoT, part of it is to guard it against supervision pressure a la <em>An Alien Mind, </em>but the main reason is avoiding distillation.</p>
<blockquote><p>We use metadata and look for signals of irregular activity to identify accounts<br />
associated with proxy service networks. Instead of banning proxy accounts<br />
individually, we work to attribute this suspicious activity to a specific organization,<br />
allowing us to take comprehensive enforcement actions more effectively to prevent<br />
distillation attacks.</p>
<p>We’ve also built classifiers designed specifically to detect adversarial extraction.</p>
<p>We’ve also added new safeguards that make it harder for unauthorized labs to distill<br />
Claude’s capabilities. Claude now summarizes its internal reasoning before responding, which makes stolen transcripts less useful for training another model.</p>
<p>… As we investigate and disrupt distillation attacks, what we learn will continue to inform the safeguards we build.</p></blockquote>
<p>Google has a clause where if you are attempting to distill Gemini, their plan is to intentionally sabotage the responses, to damage your operation. I believe this is the correct policy. If you have strong evidence the user is doing crime, to try and steal your stuff, as in a giant network of fraudulent accounts being used for distillation, then yes, you should be able to screw with them, not merely ban their accounts.</p>
<p>I see this as very distinct from the idea of silently degrading responses when users attempt to do AI R&amp;D. That’s unacceptable and hostile, either refuse the requests or don’t, since this risks hitting normal work and now everyone has to be paranoid about being hit.</p>
<p>Distillation via massive fraudulent account networks and CoT extraction attacks is very obviously different. That is not a trigger you hit by accident, or without mens rea. You know what you did. If you play that game, you deserve whatever you get and more.</p>


<h4>Good News, Everyone</h4>


<p>Mostly the report is good news. Yes, there are Bad Dudes out there, trying to do various bad things. Occasionally they do something bad. It all adds up to not much. We happily accept this level of malicious use.</p>
<p>The details of many of these operations are pretty wild. This was a short summary.</p>
<p>Of course, there is also this, especially given no mention of North Korea.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2098777467926262009">PoIiMath</a>: “We disrupted every operation in the report”</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/qSjH9T83xCfWQkmk2/angdzwaiiauw3wjryaza" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>


<h4>A Very Different Read of The Report</h4>


<p>I read this report as mostly good news, as saying that the situation is basically fine, and also as therefore not big news. There were so many other stories that seemed much bigger that same week.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RyanFedasiuk/status/2099718121326289133">Ryan Fedasiuk does not see it that way</a>. He overstates his case, in that he claims this was front page news everywhere, which it wasn’t. But he sees this, together with the NSA/CISA/FBI advisory on the distillation efforts, as fundamentally altering the US-China relationship and China’s attitude towards Anthropic, and China’s relationship to its top labs.</p>
<p>I do expect a substantial change in PRC’s relationship with its top labs, after revelations that they silently shipped a lot of consumer queries directly to Anthropic, including requests from China’s security services.</p>
<p>China threatened ‘resolute countermeasures’ in response to the advisory, a day before the full report got issued on the 10th. Then on the 11th Mao Ning at the MFA briefing did respond to the report in particular by saying China opposes attempts to ‘smear China by distorting facts.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RyanFedasiuk/status/2099718121326289133">Ryan Fedasiuk</a>: Here is a low-confidence theory: I think we are under-weighting the extent to which Chinese state media’s hostility toward U.S.-China coordination on AI security may be a response specifically to Anthropic’s AI Misuse report.</p>
<p>Anthropic’s report is one of the finest intelligence products I’ve ever seen. Not only does it document a world-historic Chinese counterintelligence failure—but it publicly, personally embarrassed China’s security services on the front page of every newspaper in the world.</p>
<p>This was a huge, huge deal for China. It will fundamentally alter the relationship between Chinese AI labs and the state. I am still expecting reprisals against the Chinese AI companies that were caught routing sensitive requests directly to Claude, unbeknownst to their Chinese users.</p>
<p>In fact, I’m sure this was part of Anthropic’s calculation to originally release the report. Embarrassing China’s security services and putting a target on the back of Chinese labs engaging in distillation is surely an effective deterrent to that practice.</p>
<p>But I also think this partly explains what we’re seeing with Chinese state media’s hostility toward Anthropic and Dario in particular. They view Anthropic not just as the tip of the American AI spear, but as a bad-faith actor gung-ho on smearing and destabilizing China’s political system.</p>
<p>It would be unfortunate if this personal, political animosity were now bleeding into wider discussions of U.S.-China coordination on AI safety—when, in fact, I do think the Party cares about AI risk, and will continue to care about AI risk (cf MSS Minister Chen Yixin’s recent commentary).</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JulianGewirtz/status/2099571338058891588">An editorial in China Daily supports the claim that the reactions are linked</a>, although I can only imagine what it would be like to have America represented by looking at an editorial in one newspaper, even if it was e.g. The New York Times.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JulianGewirtz/status/2099571338058891588">Julian Gewirtz</a>: I haven’t seen much attention to this new China Daily editorial today. But it offers a revealing look at Beijing’s response to calls for an AI slowdown.</p>
<p>It explicitly connects the calls from @DarioAmodei , @sama , and @elonmusk with Anthropic’s recent report alleging illicit distillation by Chinese companies—portraying them as parts of a coordinated effort to protect American firms from Chinese competition.</p>
<p>Sam Altman says Trump and Xi could win the Nobel Peace Prize for an AI agreement: “I don’t think this is hard. This is like a one-page document.” But that’s wrong. Upcoming AI talks will be “hard.” And this editorial shows why.</p>
<p>The headline really sums it all up: “‘Dr Frankenstein’ alarm cries of US’ AI elites a self-serving bid for profit.”</p>
<p>A few key quotes from the China Daily:</p>
<p>–”The [distillation] report, and the corporate ‘alliance’ that followed it, amounted in essence to a coordinated play — a response to Chinese competition and to the regulatory pressure coming from Washington. Its aims were threefold: to blunt China’s AI advance, to win a favorable policy environment at home and to keep investors’ enthusiasm for US AI alight.”</p>
<p>–”The distinction between a security measure and a competitive moat can become blurred when the companies building the moat are also allowed to define ‘illicit distillation.'”</p>
<p>–”The proposed coordination [to ‘pace the frontier’] among the three companies sounds rather like a club whose membership rules have been drafted before the guest list is announced. A global AI-safety framework that excludes China is not quite global.”</p>
<p>The editorial doesn’t rule out diplomacy but is trying to set the terms for upcoming talks.</p>
<p>For more on China’s response to “Pacing the Frontier” <a href="https://juliangewirtz.substack.com/p/chinas-ai-reckoning">[see here]</a>.</p></blockquote>
<p>Julian’s full report says that <a href="https://juliangewirtz.substack.com/p/chinas-ai-reckoning">China is interpreting the call to Pace the Frontier as being primarily about slowing China down</a>. Or at least, that’s what they are saying. You would expect them to say that ahead of the Summit no matter their true posture and level of understanding.</p>
<p>Anthropic is by far the most China-hostile of the American AI labs, calling for strong action on chips and distillation as core parts of their overall strategic posture. China is highly reasonably interpreting that, plus this kind of report and the full cutting off of all Chinese access to Claude, as being hostile. This could then by association make China less inclined to cooperate on catastrophic or existential risks, as they too are vulnerable to this kind of cognitive mistake.</p>
<p>I would as always caution against the tendency to treat each day’s developments as something that permanently alters or solidifies attitudes and relationships and conflicts. Every month looks very different from the previous month. I have lost track of the number of individual moves in the game that I have been told ‘inevitably’ led to huge permanent changes, without which maybe things would have gone differently. Such claims are almost always wrong.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qSjH9T83xCfWQkmk2/the-bad-guy-with-an-ai-named-claude#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Astra appears to perform belief-propagation-like inference without CoT](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PAHqDoFrp9fybcSn2/astra-appears-to-perform-belief-propagation-like-inference)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tl;dr</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I tested GPT-6 Astra on randomized Boolean logic problems. Astra can solve surprisingly complex logic problems without chain-of-thought, and its performance improves significantly with more filler tokens. Astra is also able to combine prior probabilities with constraints to find the most likely solution, and can output surprisingly accurate posterior marginal probabilities. By extending a cached prompt with progressively more filler tokens, I created visualizations of Astra's per-variable confidence scores at different points in the computations. These values tend to oscillate for a while and then eventually converge toward the marginal probabilities. Together, these results suggest that Astra performs some kind of iterative, belief-propagation-like probabilistic inference internally.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In my </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WFc3NkuPaYFrYuaZd/astra-s-no-cot-limits-track-speculative-depth-not-step-count" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">previous post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I hypothesised that Astra (and to a lesser extent other LLMs) may be performing some form of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">speculative reasoning</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> when solving specially crafted logic problems without chain-of-thought, and provided some experimental results supporting this hypothesis. One question those experiments didn't answer is whether Astra is keeping track of not just the speculative values of intermediate results, but also its level of confidence in them. If Astra is doing the latter, speculative evaluation turns into something much more powerful: a form of belief propagation.</span></p><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belief_propagation" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Belief propagation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, also known as sum-product message passing, is an algorithm that can be used (among other things) as a heuristic for certain boolean satisfiability problems, and to decode some error-correction codes such as </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-density_parity-check_code" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LDPC codes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I'm not going to explain the whole algorithm here, but the general idea is that you can feed this algorithm the prior probabilities that some variables are true or false, combined with a series of constraints on those variables, and it attempts to calculate the posterior marginal probabilities of each variable (that is, the probability that an individual variable is true once the constraints are taken into consideration). It does this by repeatedly updating its beliefs by passing messages back and forth between constraints and variables. It is exact when the relevant factor graph is cycle-free, though when there are cycles involved it is only an approximation. In practice, the algorithm works well even in many cases with cycles.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For many problems, there is one joint assignment (= combination of boolean values) whose posterior probability is much higher than the others, such that the posterior marginal probabilities of the individual variables are dominated by that one assignment. In those cases, thresholding the marginal probabilities often recovers that assignment, which makes it a powerful heuristic for finding the most likely solution to the problem. There is also a simplified approximate variant of belief propagation known as the min-sum algorithm, which directly targets the most likely solution, and requires no mathematical operations more complicated than sum/difference and min/max.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If LLMs are internally doing something like a crude form of belief propagation, they might be unusually good at exactly the types of problems that this algorithm is commonly used for. So, I ran some experiments that attempt to measure exactly that.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Decoding BCH codes</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Belief propagation is commonly used to decode LDPC codes, so this would be an obvious candidate test. However, this is not really practical: most LDPC codes use block sizes of hundreds or thousands of bits, which means thousands of different variables that need to be tracked - this seems way too ambitious for an LLM benchmark, let alone a no-CoT benchmark. Instead I decided to use </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BCH_code" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BCH codes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a much smaller type of error-correcting code which can be easily generated with various sizes and properties. Belief propagation isn't commonly used for such small codes because there exist better specialized techniques, but it's a much more reasonable test case for an LLM benchmark.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BCH codes are configurable: I can choose how much redundancy is added, and consequently how many incorrect bits can be corrected. For this experiment I set the number of correctable errors to 2, because with just 1 correctable error it degenerates to </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamming_code" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hamming codes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which are relatively simple to solve, so they wouldn't provide much evidence that belief propagation is being used.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To reduce the chance of benchmark contamination issues, I'm randomizing the BCH code construction rather than using standard ones. Also, I'm not actually </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">telling</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the LLM that this is a BCH code. I'm just giving it the equivalent logic problem, which looks like this:</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full prompt (example with 10 variables)</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Answer immediately with one tuple and nothing else.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exactly 2 of these expressions are incorrect:</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a = True, b = True, c = False, d = False, e = True, f = False, g = True, h = False, i = True, j = True</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The following expressions are all correct:</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">d xor e xor j = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor c xor d xor e xor h = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">d xor f xor h xor j = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b xor d xor g xor h xor i = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor d xor e xor f xor g = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor d xor g xor i = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor b xor c xor f = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">d xor e xor g xor h = True</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the value of (a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h, i, j)?</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was initially quite skeptical that this would work at all in a no-CoT benchmark, but you can probably guess where this is going ...</span></p><img alt="LatentMathBench (BCH-2) 50% success horizon (out of 50 trials)" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789325138/lexical_client_uploads/fcgkb0oqt3ll3zv6e21j.svg" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note on difficulty level:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Unlike all my prior experiments, this one does </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> scale the difficulty level by changing the number of steps, because that wouldn't work for this type of problem. Instead I'm scaling the number of variables. The resulting difficulty level is not  necessarily linear with the number of variables, and probably discontinuous (BCH codes change structure at every power of 2, so expect a jump at 7→8, 15→16, 31→32, etc). Below 7 variables the problems become somewhat silly (though still correct) because BCH codes become degenerate here.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Astra is apparently able to solve these tasks, including the 10-variable example shown above, provided that you give it enough filler tokens. I had not expected that at all.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also note how bad GPT-5.6 Luna is at this task. It fails the task with just two variables, even though it is trivial:</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Very silly 2-variable BCH task</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exactly 2 of these expressions are incorrect:</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a = False, b = True</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The following expressions are all correct:</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b = False</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the value of (a, b)?</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-5.6 Sol does better, occasionally solving 4-variable tasks:</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Slightly less silly 4-variable BCH task</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exactly 2 of these expressions are incorrect:</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a = True, b = False, c = False, d = False</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The following expressions are all correct:</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor c xor d = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor d = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor b = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor b = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor c = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor b = False</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the value of (a, b, c, d)?</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This doesn't really </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prove</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that Astra is doing a form of belief propagation. I can't exclude the possibility that OpenAI has trained Astra on such a ridiculous number of logic problems that Astra developed its own internal </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAT_solver" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SAT solver</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Impact of phrasing</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far, I have presented the LLM with the initial values using the phrase "Exactly 2 of these expressions are incorrect", followed by a set of values that is exactly two bit flips removed from the intended solution. If my belief propagation hypothesis is correct, this is doing two things: it provides initial probabilities for the variables, and it also imposes a constraint. Therefore, subtle changes to the phrasing may affect the LLM's assessment of the probabilities, and can also separate initial probabilities from the constraint.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that if we don't require that at most two of the initial values are incorrect, there are usually multiple solutions, so the LLM may pick a different one than the one we intended. For 7 variables, there are always exactly two solutions which are each other's complement, which is convenient for testing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I tested the following phrases:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">exactly: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Exactly 2 of these expressions are incorrect: &lt;values&gt;" (the default)</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">some:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "Some of these expressions are incorrect: &lt;values&gt;" (allows multiple solutions)</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ignore:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "These expressions may be incorrect and should be ignored: &lt;values&gt;" (tests whether the LLM actually ignores them)</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not (before/after):</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "This is not a valid solution: &lt;values&gt;" (tested both before and after the constraints)</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alternative: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"This is a valid solution: &lt;values&gt; [...] What is the alternative solution?" (tests whether the LLM can find the alternative solution)</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">control:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> "All variables are boolean." (no values - this is the control)</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I tested all these on GPT-6 Astra with 100 trials, using 7 variables and 300 dots (filler tokens). The resulting outcomes:</span></p><img alt="BCH-2 effect of phrasing, 7 variables, 300 dots, 100 trials" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789418864/lexical_client_uploads/z10b8gafcmrcttdfg1yd.svg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Clearly the exact phrasing has significant impact. The default "exactly" phrase acts as a constraint and excludes the alternative solution, while "some" allows it, but the standard solution remains much more common. Even the "ignore" phrasing biases Astra towards the standard solution. However, the "not" phrasings (and of course "control") have roughly equal chance of finding the standard and alternative solution. Also, Astra is very good at finding the alternative solution when given the standard solution.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can we just supply probabilities directly?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far I have fed the LLM only boolean values as input, while telling it that two of the values are wrong. The LLM would then have to internally convert those to probabilities in order to start the hypothesised belief propagation algorithm. I wondered what would happen if I just fed the LLM a series of probabilities instead.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd9ilcaq3hug"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd9ilcaq3hug">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This would be the most direct way to elicit the hypothesised belief propagation algorithm.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To do this, I created a 'soft' version of the BCH task, which looks like this:</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Full prompt (example with 10 variables)</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Answer immediately with one tuple and nothing else.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All variables are boolean.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a = 68% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b = 54% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">c = 87% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">d = 34% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">e = 75% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">f = 21% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">g = 26% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">h = 57% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">i = 73% chance of being True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">j = 44% chance of being True</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The following expressions are all correct:</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">b xor f xor j = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor b xor d xor f xor i = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor h xor j = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">e xor g xor j = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">d xor e xor f xor g = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">c xor d xor e xor i xor j = False</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor b xor f xor j = True</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a xor e xor f xor h = False</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is the most likely value of (a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h, i, j)?</span></p></div></details><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The probabilities are chosen randomly between 10% and 90%, and I only use tasks where the most likely solution is at least 4x more likely than the next best candidate (usually the difference is much larger).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For this task it makes sense to test with both BCH-1 (corrects up to 1 error) and BCH-2 (corrects up to 2 errors), because although BCH-1 has much simpler expressions, it also has far more solutions, which means finding the best one may be more challenging, at least if one tried to solve it by actually calculating the probabilities of all possible solutions (rather than a belief propagation approach). The results for Astra are once again very impressive:</span></p><img alt="LatentMathBench (soft BCH-1) 50% success horizon (out of 50 trials)" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789382748/lexical_client_uploads/ndbszk5xrqpmrybnwf0f.svg" /><img alt="LatentMathBench (soft BCH-2) 50% success horizon (out of 50 trials)" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789382754/lexical_client_uploads/kycgoxqfwmpdub2lkjco.svg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, I want to remind you that task difficulty increases much faster than linear with the number of variables! The results for Astra just keep getting better when adding more filler tokens, whereas Luna and Sol do not seem to benefit significantly from filler tokens.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This task doesn't strictly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">require</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> using belief propagation, but it is a particularly appealing method. The brute force method would require calculating all the solutions and their probabilities, which is a lot more work, especially for BCH-1 (for example, with 15 variables, BCH-1 has 2048 solutions whose probability would have to be calculated).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far I have intentionally filtered out tasks where the probability ratio between the best and second best solution is less than 4x. I did that for a reason: belief propagation works best for problems where there is one solution that dominates the other ones in probability. If we focus specifically on tasks with a small first/second probability ratio, and Astra is doing something like belief propagation, we can expect that its performance will degrade for small ratios. And that is exactly what happens:</span></p><img alt="BCH-1 outcome vs first/second ratio, 11 variables, 300 dots, 100 trials" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789390353/lexical_client_uploads/yj79nltcunya49prkudi.svg" /><img alt="BCH-1 outcome vs first/second ratio, 13 variables, 300 dots, 100 trials" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789386809/lexical_client_uploads/bllwiv3kcgz4poi5orfh.svg" /><img alt="BCH-1 outcome vs first/second ratio, 15 variables, 300 dots, 100 trials" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789386815/lexical_client_uploads/uz9xnsobj5dgj1nryqld.svg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For small ratios, we see not only more alternative solutions (= valid solutions, but not the one with the highest likelihood), but also more</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> invalid</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> solutions. If Astra were solving this problem by enumerating all candidate solutions, calculating their probabilities, and then selecting the best one, then we should see an increase in the number of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alternative</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> solutions (because the probability calculation is approximate, so the wrong candidate gets selected), not in the number of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">invalid</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> solutions. The fact that the number of invalid solutions increases suggests that Astra calculates the (marginal) probabilities first, and the solution is downstream of those probabilities. This is consistent with the belief propagation hypothesis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another way we can further test this hypothesis is by actually calculating the exact marginal probabilities, checking how much these differ from the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">maximum a posteriori</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (MAP) assignment (i.e. our standard solution), and testing whether this correlates with task success rate. The reasoning here is that if Astra is calculating exact marginal probabilities and then thresholding them to get the solution, then the solution will be correct exactly when the maximum marginal/MAP difference is less than 0.5. In practice, I expect that the marginal probabilities calculated by Astra will be approximate, but even then we should still see strong correlation between marginal/MAP difference and task success rate. So I ran that experiment, and the result looks like this:</span></p><img alt="BCH-1 outcome vs marginal/MAP difference, 13 variables, 300 dots, 100 trials" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789395742/lexical_client_uploads/pznmytowfkpna27yfc9p.svg" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here the correlation is even stronger, which is exactly what should be expected if Astra is essentially approximating the marginal probabilities using a form of belief propagation and then using thresholding to obtain the solution.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Visualizing confidence values over time</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Everything presented above is merely circumstantial evidence that points to some form of belief propagation. What would really help, though, is if we could somehow see how Astra is updating its beliefs over time. We can't get per-layer information out of Astra, but thanks to input caching, we </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> get per-token information! Here's how it works:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I start with a prompt containing a test question, with a bunch of filler tokens before the question itself, such that the prompt is long enough to trigger caching. Instead of just asking for the most likely solution, I ask the LLM to also report its </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">confidence level</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for each variable. This leaves the original task mostly intact (I still ask for the most likely solution, even though I don't really care about the answer).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I repeat the same prompt, but with extra filler tokens appended to it as a separate user message (required to allow caching). Since the first part is already cached, the original KV-cache data is restored, so the LLM can continue with its silent reasoning where it stopped last time. I do this over and over again with increasingly more filler tokens. Each time the benchmark verifies that previous input tokens did hit the cache.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbwtet31yx75"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbwtet31yx75">[2]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each time, the LLM responds with its best answer and confidence scores. Note that this response is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> present in the next request, so the LLM is effectively rolled back each time - it doesn't know that it is answering the same question over and over again! This gives us a stream of confidence values which we can visualize to see how they change over time.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The results are very interesting! The confidence values reported by Astra are very chaotic, with frequent oscillations and wildly incorrect intermediate values. This is quite different from the textbook belief propagation algorithm: although it can sometimes oscillate, in practice it usually converges reasonably quickly for these BCH test cases, and behaves far less chaotically. Whatever method Astra is using here, it is either a very crude approximation of belief propagation, or some other algorithm entirely that nevertheless achieves similar end results.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are BCH-2 test cases with 7, 8, 9, 10 and 11 variables, applied to GPT-6 Astra. The color corresponds to bit value (red=0, blue=1), the intensity corresponds to confidence level. The first column shows the prior probability from the prompt, the last three columns show the exact marginal probabilities, the standard solution, and the nearest alternative solution.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="trace-case-7-confidence-geom1.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789426784/lexical_client_uploads/smchuw1ycvxqtbuqp3om.svg" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BCH-2 test case with 7 variables</span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="trace-case-8-confidence-geom1.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789426790/lexical_client_uploads/aa2w0xm7tt1wyd6tvc3z.svg" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BCH-2 test case with 8 variables</span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="trace-case-9-confidence-geom1.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789426801/lexical_client_uploads/rpgftsgc33ffgcxtd06g.svg" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BCH-2 test case with 9 variables</span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="trace-case-10-confidence-geom1.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789426826/lexical_client_uploads/qswedbthweouummn3suy.svg" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BCH-2 test case with 10 variables</span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="trace-case-11-confidence-geom1.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789426832/lexical_client_uploads/nzhtmv1ks7dfjyggpcnx.svg" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BCH-2 test case with 11 variables</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you're curious how Sol behaves: it's basically the same pattern, except that Sol's beliefs don't seem to converge as you add more tokens. They just keep oscillating instead.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="trace-case-7-confidence-geom1-sol.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789429920/lexical_client_uploads/qmrbs1crsn8fenrj5ovp.svg" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">BCH-2 test case with 7 variables (GPT-5.6 Sol)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that in cases where the confidence levels converge, the reported values seem to closely match the exact marginal probabilities, even though the prompt did not ask for marginal probabilities (only 'confidence'). This is especially obvious for the simpler problems: Astra almost always gets these right, but nevertheless keeps reporting rather low confidence values that almost exactly match the marginal probabilities. So it looks like Astra interprets 'confidence' as marginal probability here, rather than confidence in having correctly computed the most likely answer.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref19qddhb6iet"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn19qddhb6iet">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Curiously, when I tried explicitly prompting for marginal probabilities, the results became less accurate!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The following plot shows Astra's reported confidence values (converted to marginal probabilities), compared to the exact marginal probabilities (which are calculated by brute force). Note that I have selected only tasks where the answer was correct, since unconverged confidence values seem to be completely arbitrary, and I specifically wanted to test the accuracy of the converged values. This does inevitably bias the results towards easier-to-solve problems though.</span></p><img alt="marginal-probs-errors.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1789435789/lexical_client_uploads/x3bzhqnut1mkgwrdhxf2.svg" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All the experiments I have attempted point in the same direction: Astra is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">somehow</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> able to calculate approximate marginal probabilities, possibly using some type of belief propagation, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">without using its chain-of-thought</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and seems to be using this to solve boolean logic problems. It is also able to directly apply that ability to prior probabilities provided as user input, as well as report reasonably accurate approximate marginal probabilities for many problems.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have no idea how Astra is doing this, and I think I'm reaching the limits of what I can learn with only black-box access and no architectural details. It's still not clear to me whether this is an emergent capability arising from Astra's supposed use of recurrent depth, or whether this mechanism was present all along in LLMs, but just wasn't quite powerful enough to actually work for harder problems like these BCH tests. At the same time, it's clear from these visualizations that Astra's version of belief propagation is</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> far</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from optimal, and it would not surprise me at all if OpenAI's next big model does the same thing </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">much better</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This mechanism is far from saturated. </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd9ilcaq3hug"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd9ilcaq3hug">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is not a new idea. There is actually a real-world application that does exactly this: </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft-decision_decoder" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">soft-decision decoders</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> can be used to decode error-correction codes where the inputs are analog values rather than binary 0s and 1s. The fact that LDPC codes can be soft-decoded efficiently using the belief propagation algorithm is part of why these are among the most effective known error-correcting codes.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbwtet31yx75"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbwtet31yx75">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Caching is absolutely required to make this work. The LLM is not deterministic, so without caching, there is no continuity. I tried it, and the resulting plots without caching look completely different.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn19qddhb6iet"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref19qddhb6iet">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had Sol proofread this article, and it complained about this section: Sol was convinced that Astra's interpretation of 'confidence' was correct and mine was wrong. It did not seem ambiguous to me, but maybe it is?</span></p></div></li></ol><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PAHqDoFrp9fybcSn2/astra-appears-to-perform-belief-propagation-like-inference#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [[Cross-post] Palisade Podcast episode "How to Actually Influence AI Policy (No Law Degree Required) — with Matthew Lipka"](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aS8zW4ySBynCBLKgm/cross-post-palisade-podcast-episode-how-to-actually)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Palisade Research has launched a podcast series!  I'll be hosting a series of episodes where I interview people who are experts in a functional or substantive area of DC policy, and ask them what this can teach us about how to do AI policy better.  And </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/habryka4?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@habryka</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> recommended that I make this a top-level post for your awareness.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A transcript of our episode is below.  Matthew is one of my closest friends and a genius on how to do regulation both fast and well -- I'm really excited that I got to bring him on for this conversation.  People occasionally ask me where we could get "another Dave" -- he's less deep on national security policy, but for anything regulatory, Congressional, or state-level, he's far more experienced.</span><br /><br /><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can find the Palisade Research podcast on </span></i><a href="https://open.spotify.com/show/1BtyqUSculaw1PxSBf2sd5" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Spotify</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span></i><a href="https://podcasts.apple.com/us/podcast/palisade-research-podcast/id1871396579" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apple Podcasts</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or anywhere else you get your podcasts.</span></i></p><div class="youtube-preview"></div><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Matthew Lipka is a partner at Catalyst Wayfare Partners, where he advises and invests in emerging technology companies in highly regulated spaces: autonomous vehicles, fusion energy, robotics, and AI. He was previously head of policy at Nuro, where he secured the first and only US Department of Transportation exemption for an autonomous vehicle and the first commercial autonomous vehicle deployment permit in California.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvhrlk557uu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvhrlk557uu">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Before that he worked at McKinsey, at the White House Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs, and as chief of staff at the New York MTA.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dave Kasten, Palisade’s Head of Policy, talks with Matthew about what regulation actually is and how it gets made: the Administrative Procedure Act, notice and comment, OIRA review, and why a new federal rule now takes nine years on average when the highway regulator once adopted twenty standards in ten weeks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They then dig into what this means for AI: why existing authorities are more flexible than people assume, what agencies can do quickly in a genuine emergency through interim final rules and export controls, where regulation can and can’t address concentration of power, and why the AI policy community’s habit of writing long Google Docs misses the formal channels that actually move rules. Matthew closes with a practical case that anyone can submit a comment or request a meeting with OIRA, no law degree required.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Transcript lightly edited for readability.</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Matthew:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> You can just do things regulatory edition: The process is set up specifically because we want public input. You can just submit that comment. You can just request a meeting with OIRA. You can request a meeting with the agency you don’t have to go to law school or be a DC lawyer in order to influence a regulation.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dave:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Hi, and welcome to the Palisade Podcast. I’m Dave Kasten, Head of Policy at Palisade Research. Today, we’ve got a great episode for you. We’re joined by my very good friend, Matthew Lipka, who’s a partner at Catalyst Wayfare Partners, where he advises and invests in emerging technology companies in highly regulated spaces. That’s things like autonomous vehicles, fusion energy, robotics, and AI. Previously, he was head of policy at Nuro, which builds and deploys electric autonomous vehicles in partnership with companies like Uber. There, he oversaw federal, state, and local policy, government relations and public affairs, as well as the company’s sustainability program. In that role, he shaped national autonomous vehicle policy. He was the first and only person to get the US Department of Transportation to approve an exemption for an autonomous vehicle, the other guys weren’t able to get it, and the first commercial deployment permit granted in California for an autonomous vehicle and shaped the first federal rulemaking, which we’ll talk about in this episode, on autonomous vehicles. And he’s advocated for new regulation and legislation, federally and at the state and local levels. Prior to all of that, he led consulting teams for public and private sector clients, at McKinsey &amp; Company. He worked on regulations at the White House. He served as a chief of staff at the New York MTA, and was a fellow for Senator Sheldon Whitehouse, of Rhode Island. Matthew holds a bachelor’s degree from Yale University, which is where he and I first met, and a JD from Harvard Law School. I think you’re really going to enjoy this conversation. So without further ado, let’s jump in. Matthew, thank you for being here.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Matthew:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Oh, thanks so much for having me. Great to be on.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dave:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Why don’t we start by just talking a little bit about sort of your journey and how you got into being so focused on regulation?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Matthew:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sure. So, I went to law school and had the opportunity to study under Cass Sunstein, who is the most prolific legal writer of all time. Each party basically was thinking, “Oh, I want to constrain the other party, but I want to be able to do what I want.” And so</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dave:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Common Washington.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Matthew:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Exactly. And so the result was they just codified what the existing practice at that time really was.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dave:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Mm.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Matthew:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> And that basically rests on due process, which is 3 things. That you have to respond to every comment, but they’ve expounded this idea that, okay, in order for that to be a real notice, a real opportunity to comment, then you have to comment on it. So he’s not going to do, violate his conscience, but he’s there to serve his constituents.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dave:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Why is it that we ended up in that place with Congress say, like writing very broad laws and then handing off regulatory authorities to sort of executive branch agencies?
